System-wide API tracing may be combined with trace types that are\n\nalways system-wide such as\n--trace=wddm\n.\nTo trace a DX11 or DX12 target application, it must gain the system focus, the\n\nuser must either click on the application window or using Alt+Tab to select it.\nFor example, to trace multiple DX12 applications with PIX markers and GPU\n\nworkload trace as well as WDDM events for the next 20 seconds, run the command:\nnsys profile --trace=dx12-annotations,wddm --dx12-gpu-workload=individual\n\n--duration=20\nthen click each of the target applications’ windows to give them focus.\nTo trace a Vulkan target application, it must be launched after the\nnsys\nprofile\ncommand has been executed.\nOpening Command Line Results Files for Visualization\n\nOpen CLI results in GUI\nThe CLI will generate an .nsys-rep file after analysis is complete. This file can\n\nbe opened in any GUI that is the same version or a more recent version.\nThe opening of really large, multi-gigabyte, .nsys-rep files may take up all of\n\nthe memory on the host computer and lock up the system. This will be fixed in a\n\nlater version.\nImporting Windows ETL files\nFor Windows targets, ETL files captured with Xperf or the\nlog.cmd\ncommand\n\nsupplied with GPUView in the Windows Performance Toolkit can be imported to\n\ncreate reports as if they were captured with Nsight Systems’s “WDDM trace” and\n\n“Custom ETW trace” features. Simply choose the .etl file from the Import dialog\n\nto convert it to a .nsys-rep file.\nHandling Application Launchers (mpirun, deepspeed, etc)\n\nNsight Systems has built-in API trace support for various communication APIs,\n\nsuch as MPI, OpenSHMEM, UCX, NCCL and NVSHMEM.\n\nTo execute respective programs on multiple different machines (compute nodes),\n\nusually launchers are used, e.g.\nmpirun\n/\nmpiexec\n(MPI),\nshmemrun\n/\noshrun\n(OpenSHMEM),\nsrun\n(SLURM) or\ndeepspeed\n.\nIn\nsingle-node profiling\nsessions, the Nsight Systems CLI can be prefixed before\n\nthe program (binary) or the launcher. In the latter case, the execution of the\n\nlauncher will also be profiled and all processes will be recorded into the same\n\nreport file, e.g for mpirun\nnsys\nprofile\n[nsys\nargs]\nmpirun\n[mpirun\nargs]\n...\nIn\nmulti-node profiling\nsessions, the Nsight Systems CLI has to be prefixed\n\nbefore the application, but not before the launcher command, e.g. for mpirun\nmpirun\n[mpirun\nargs]\nnsys\nprofile\n[nsys\nargs]\n...\nYou can use\n%q{OMPI_COMM_WORLD_RANK}\n(Open MPI),\n%q{PMI_RANK}\n(MPICH),\n%q{SLURM_PROCID}\n(Slurm) or\n%p\nin the\n-o|--output\noption to include\n\nthe rank or process ID into the report file name.\nWarning:\nAn error will occur, if several processes want to write to the same report file\n\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 136000:148000]\nrofiler-users-guide\n.\nThe nvprof transition guide for Nsight Compute can be found at\nhttps://docs.nvidia.com/nsight-compute/NsightComputeCli/index.html#nvprof-guide\n.\nAny nvprof switch not listed below is not supported by the\nnsys\nnvprof\ncommand. If disabled, the application can use {cu,cuda}Profiler{Start,Stop} to turn on/off profiling.\n-t\n--timeout\n<nanoseconds> default=\n0\n--duration=seconds\nIf greater than 0, stop the collection and kill the launched application after timeout seconds. nvprof started counting when the CUDA driver is initialized. nsys starts counting immediately.\n--cpu-profiling\non,\noff\n--sampling=cpu\nTurn on/off CPU profiling\n--openacc-profiling\non\n, off\n--trace=openacc\nto turn on\nEnable/disable recording information from the OpenACC profiling interface. Note: OpenACC profiling interface depends on the presence of the OpenACC runtime. For supported runtimes, see\nCUDA Trace\nsection of documentation\n-o\n--export-profile\n<filename>\n--output={filename}\nand/or\n--export=sqlite\nExport named file to be imported or opened in the Nsight Systems GUI. %q{ENV_VAR} in string will be replaced with the set value of the environment variable. If not set this is an error. %h in the string is replaced with the system hostname. %% in the string is replaced with %. %p in the string is not supported currently. Any other character following % is illegal. The default is report1, with the number incrementing to avoid overwriting files, in users working directory.\n-f\n--force-overwrite\n--force-overwrite=true\nForce overwriting all output files with same name.\n-h\n--help\n--help\nPrint Nsight Systems CLI help\n-V\n--version\n--version\nPrint Nsight Systems CLI version information\nNext Steps\n\nNVIDIA Visual Profiler (NVVP) and NVIDIA nvprof are deprecated. New GPUs and features will not be supported by those tools. We encourage you to make the move to Nsight Systems now. For additional information, suggestions, and rationale, see the blog series in\nOther Resources\n.\nDirect3D Trace\n\nNsight Systems has the ability to trace both the Direct3D 11 API and the Direct3D 12 API on Windows targets.\nD3D11 API trace\n\nNsight Systems can capture information about Direct3D 11 API calls made by the profiled process. This includes capturing the execution time of D3D11 API functions, performance markers, and frame durations.\nSLI Trace\nTrace SLI queries and peer-to-peer transfers of D3D11 applications. Requires SLI hardware and an active SLI profile definition in the NVIDIA console.\nD3D12 API Trace\n\nDirect3D 12 is a low-overhead 3D graphics and compute API for Microsoft Windows. Information about Direct3D 12 can be found at the\nDirect3D 12 Programming Guide\n.\nNsight Systems can capture information about Direct3D 12 usage by the profiled process. This includes capturing the execution time of D3D12 API functions, corresponding workloads executed on the GPU, performance markers, and frame durations.\nThe Command List Creation row displays time periods when command lists were being created. This enables developers to improve their application’s multi-threaded command list creation. Command list creation time period is measured between the call to\nID3D12GraphicsCommandList::Reset\nand the call to\nID3D12GraphicsCommandList::Close\n.\nThe GPU row shows a compressed view of the D3D12 queue activity, color-coded by the queue type. Expanding it will show the individual queues and their corresponding API calls.\nA Command Queue row is displayed for each D3D12 command queue created by the profiled application. The row’s header displays the queue’s running index and its type (Direct, Compute, Copy).\nThe DX12 API Memory Ops row displays all API memory operations and non-persistent resource mappings. Event ranges in the row are color-coded by the heap type they belong to (Default, Readback, Upload, Custom, or CPU-Visible VRAM), with usage warnings highlighted in yellow. A breakdown of the operations can be found by expanding the row to show rows for each individual heap type.\nThe following operations and warnings are shown:\nCalls to\nID3D12Device::CreateCommittedResource\n,\nID3D12Device4::CreateCommittedResource1\n, and\nID3D12Device8::CreateCommittedResource2\nA warning will be reported if\nD3D12_HEAP_FLAG_CREATE_NOT_ZEROED\nis not set in the method’s\nHeapFlags\nparameter\nCalls to\nID3D12Device::CreateHeap\nand\nID3D12Device4::CreateHeap1\nA warning will be reported if\nD3D12_HEAP_FLAG_CREATE_NOT_ZEROED\nis not set in the\nFlags\nfield of the method’s\npDesc\nparameter\nCalls to\nID3D12Resource::ReadFromSubResource\nA warning will be reported if the read is to a\nD3D12_CPU_PAGE_PROPERTY_WRITE_COMBINE\nCPU page or from a\nD3D12_HEAP_TYPE_UPLOAD\nresource\nCalls to\nID3D12Resource::WriteToSubResource\nA warning will be reported if the write is from a\nD3D12_CPU_PAGE_PROPERTY_WRITE_BACK\nCPU page or to a\nD3D12_HEAP_TYPE_READBACK\nresource\nCalls to\nID3D12Resource::Map\nand\nID3D12Resource::Unmap\nwill be matched into [Map, Unmap] ranges for non-persistent mappings. If a mapping range is nested, only the most external range (reference count = 1) will be shown.\nThe API row displays time periods where\nID3D12CommandQueue::ExecuteCommandLists\nwas called. The GPU Workload row displays time periods where workloads were executed by the GPU. The workload’s type (Graphics, Compute, Copy, etc.) is displayed on the bar representing the workload’s GPU execution.\nIn addition, you can see the PIX command queue CPU-side performance markers, GPU-side performance markers and the GPU Command List performance markers, each in their row.\nClicking on a GPU workload highlights the corresponding\nID3D12CommandQueue::ExecuteCommandLists\n,\nID3D12GraphicsCommandList::Reset\nand\nID3D12GraphicsCommandList::Close\nAPI\ncalls, and vice versa.\nDetecting which CPU thread was blocked by a fence can be difficult in complex apps that run tens of CPU threads. The timeline view displays the 3 operations involved:\nThe CPU thread pushing a signal command and fence value into the command queue. This is displayed on the DX12 Synchronization sub-row of the calling thread.\nThe GPU executing that command, setting the fence value and signaling the fence. This is displayed on the GPU Queue Synchronization sub-row.\nThe CPU thread calling a Win32 wait API to block-wait until the fence is signaled. This is displayed on the Thread’s OS runtime libraries row.\nClicking one of these will highlight it and the corresponding other two calls.\nNsight Systems D3D12 trace captures D3D12 Work Graphs dispatch calls to DispatchGraph and time boundaries of the GPU execution of the work graph.\nThe DX12 API row displays\nID3D12GraphicsCommandList10::DispatchGraph\ncalls. The GPU PIX Markers row marks graph execution by the GPU with a custom marker captioned “D3D12 graph execution”.\nWDDM Queues\n\nThe Windows Display Driver Model (WDDM) architecture uses queues to send work packets from the CPU to the GPU. Each D3D device in each process is associated with one or more contexts. Graphics, compute, and copy commands that the profiled application uses are associated with a context, batched in a command buffer, and pushed into the relevant queue associated with that context.\nNsight Systems can capture the state of these queues during the trace session.\nEnabling the “Collect additional range of ETW events” option will also capture extended DxgKrnl events from the\nMicrosoft-Windows-DxgKrnl\nprovider, such as context status, allocations, sync wait, signal events, etc.\nA command buffer in a WDDM queues may have one the following types:\nRender\nDeferred\nSystem\nMMIOFlip\nWait\nSignal\nDevice\nSoftware\nIt may also be marked as a Present buffer, indicating that the application has finished rendering and requests to display the source surface.\nSee the Microsoft documentation for the WDDM architecture and the DXGKETW_QUEUE_PACKET_TYPE enumeration.\nTo retain the .etl trace files captured, so that they can be viewed in other tools (e.g. GPUView), change the “Save ETW log files in project folder” option under “Profile Behavior” in Nsight Systems’s global Options dialog. The .etl files will appear in the same folder as the .nsys-rep file, accessible by right-clicking the report in the Project Explorer and choosing “Show in Folder…”. Data collected from each ETW provider will appear in its own .etl file, and an additional .etl file named “Report XX-Merged-*.etl”, containing the events from all captured sources, will be created as well.\nWDDM HW Scheduler\n\nWhen GPU Hardware Scheduling is enabled in Windows 10 or newer version, the Windows Display Driver Model (WDDM) uses the DxgKrnl ETW provider to expose report of NVIDIA GPUs’ hardware scheduling context switches.\nNsight Systems can capture these context switch events, and display under the GPUs in the timeline rows titled WDDM HW Scheduler - [HW Queue type]. The ranges under each queue will show the process name and PID assoicated with the GPU work during the time period.\nThe events will be captured if GPU Hardware Scheduling is enabled in the Windows System Display settings, and “Collect WDDM Trace” is enabled in the Nsight Systems Project Settings.\nVulkan API Trace\n\nVulkan Overview\n\nVulkan is a low-overhead, cross-platform 3D graphics and compute API, targeting a wide variety of devices from PCs to mobile phones and embedded platforms. The Vulkan API is defined by the Khronos Group. Information about Vulkan and the Khronos Group can be found at the\nKhronos Vulkan Site\n.\nNsight Systems can capture information about Vulkan usage by the profiled process. This includes capturing the execution time of Vulkan API functions, corresponding GPU workloads, debug util labels, and frame durations. Vulkan profiling is supported on both Windows and x86 Linux operating systems.\nThe Command Buffer Creation row displays time periods when command buffers were being created. This enables developers to improve their application’s multi-threaded command buffer creation. Command buffer creation time period is measured between the call to\nvkBeginCommandBuffer\nand the call to\nvkEndCommandBuffer\n.\nA Queue row is displayed for each Vulkan queue created by the profiled application. The API sub-row displays time periods where\nvkQueueSubmit\nwas called. The GPU Workload sub-row displays time periods where workloads were executed by the GPU.\nIn addition, you can see\nVulkan debug util labels\non both the CPU and the GPU.\nClicking on a GPU workload highlights the corresponding\nvkQueueSubmit\ncall, and vice versa.\nThe Vulkan Memory Operations row contains an aggregation of all the Vulkan host-side memory operations, such as host-blocking writes and reads or non-persistent map-unmap ranges.\nThe row is separated into sub-rows by heap index and memory type - the tooltip for each row and the ranges inside show the\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 152000:160000]\nries are\nIDXGISwapChainX::Present\ncalls.\nThe Vulkan frame boundaries are\nvkQueuePresentKHR\ncalls.\nThe timing of the actual calls to the frame boundary calls can be seen in the blue bar at the bottom of the CPU frame duration row\nThe GPU Frame Duration row displays the time measured between\nThe start time of the first GPU workload execution of this frame.\nThe start time of the first GPU workload execution of the next frame.\nReflex SDK\nNVIDIA Reflex SDK is a series of NVAPI calls that allow applications to integrate the Ultra Low Latency driver feature more directly into their game to further optimize synchronization between simulation and rendering stages and lower the latency between user input and final image rendering. For more details about Reflex SDK, see\nReflex SDK Site\n.\nNsight Systems will automatically capture NVAPI functions when either Direct3D 11, Direct3D 12, or Vulkan API trace are enabled.\nThe Reflex SDK row displays timeline ranges for the following types of latency markers:\nRenderSubmit.\nSimulation.\nPresent.\nDriver.\nOS Render Queue.\nGPU Render.\nPerformance Warnings row\nThis row shows performance warnings and common pitfalls that are automatically detected based on the enabled capture types. Warnings are reported for:\nETW performance warnings\nVulkan calls to\nvkQueueSubmit\nand D3D12 calls to\nID3D12CommandQueue::ExecuteCommandList\nthat take a longer time to execute than the total time of the GPU workloads they generated\nD3D12 Memory Operation warnings\nUsage of Vulkan API functions that may adversely affect performance\nCreation of a Vulkan device with memory zeroing, whether by physical device default or manually\nVulkan command buffer barrier which can be combined or removed, such as subsequent barriers or read-to-read barriers\nFrame Health\n\nThe Frame Health row displays actions that took significantly a longer time during the current frame, compared to the median time of the same actions executed during the surrounding 19-frames. This is a great tool for detecting the reason for frame time stuttering. Such actions may be: shader compilation, present, memory mapping, and more. Nsight Systems measures the accumulated time of such actions in each frame. For example: calculating the accumulated time of shader compilations in each frame and comparing it to the accumulated time of shader compilations in the surrounding 19 frames.\nExample of a Vulkan frame health row:\nWindows GPU Memory Utilization\n\nEach GPU has two rows detailing its memory utilization:\nGPU VRAM\n, showing\n\nthe memory consumed on the device, and\nGPU WDDM SYSMEM\n, showing the memory\n\nconsumed on the host computer RAM.\nThese rows show a green-colored line graph for the memory budget for this memory\n\nsegment, and an orange-colored line graph for the actual amount of memory used.\n\nNote that these graphs are scaled to fit the highest value enconutered, as\n\nindicated by the “Y axis” value in the row header. You can use the vertical zoom\n\nslider in the top-right of the timeline view to make the row taller and view the\n\ngraph in more detail.\nNote that the value in the GPU VRAM row is not the same as the CUDA kernel\n\nmemory allocation graph, see\nCUDA GPU Memory Allocation Graph\nfor that\n\nfunctionality.\nThe GPU VRAM row also has several child rows, accessed by expanding the row in\n\nthe tree view\nVidMm Device Suspension\nThis row displays time ranges when the GPU memory manager suspended all memory\n\ntransfer operations, pending the completion of a single memory transfer.\nDemoted Memory\nThis row displays the amount of VRAM that was demoted from GPU local memory to\n\nnon-local memory (possibly due to exceeding the VRAM budget) as a blue-colored\n\nline graph. High amounts of demoted memory could be indicative of video memory\n\nleaks or poor memory management. Note that the Demoted memory row is scaled to\n\nits highest value, similar to the GPU VRAM and GPU WDDM SYSMEM rows.\nResource Allocations\nThis row shows markers indicating resource allocation events. VRAM resources are\n\nshown as green markers while SYSMEM resources are shown in gray. Hovering over a\n\nmarker or selecting it in the\nEvents view\nwill display\n\nall the allocation parameters as well as the call stack that led to the\n\nallocation event.\nResource Migrations\nThis row displays a breakdown of resources’ movement between VRAM and SYSMEM,\n\nfocusing on resource evictions. The main row shows a timeline of total evicted\n\nresource memory and count as a red-colored line graph.\nEach child row displays a timeline of the status of each resource, as reflected\n\nby WDDM events related to it. If the object has been named using PIX or\nID3D11Object::SetName\n/\nID3D12Object::SetName\n, the name will be shown\n\nin the row title. Whether named or not, the row title will also show the\n\nresource dimensions, format, priority, and the memory size migrated. If the\n\nresource was migrated in parts using subresources, the row can be expanded to\n\nshow the status for each subresource at any given time.\nExpanding the row for a resource will show the individual WDDM events relevant\n\nto it and the call stacks that led to each event.\nBy default, the resources are sorted by Relevance (most / largest migrations).\n\nRight-clicking the main Resource Migrations row header allows choosing between\n\nthe following sorting options:\nRelevance\nName\nFormat\nPriority\nEarliest allocation timestamp (order of appearance on the host)\nEarliest migration timestamp (order of appearance on the device)\nThe top 5 resources are shown initially. If more than 5 resources exist, a row\n\nshowing the number of hidden resources and buttons allowing to show more or\n\nfewer of them will appear below them. Right-click this row and select “show all”\n\nor “show all collapsed” to display all the resources at once.\nMemory Transfer\nThis row shows an overview of all memory transfer operations. Device-to-host\n\ntransfers are shown in orange, host-to-device transfers are shown in green,\n\ndiscarded device memory is shown in light green, and unknown events are shown\n\nin dark gray. The height of each event marker corresponds to the amount of\n\nmemory that the event affected. Hovering over the marker will show the exact amount.\nExpanding the row will show a breakdown of the events by each specific type.\nVertical Synchronization\n\nThe VSYNC rows display when the monitor’s vertical synchronizations occur.\nOpenMP Trace\n\nNsight Systems for Linux is capable of capturing information about OpenMP events. This functionality is built on the OpenMP Tools Interface (OMPT), full support is available only for runtime libraries supporting tools interface defined in OpenMP 5.0 or greater.\nAs an example, LLVM OpenMP runtime library partially implements tools interface. If you use PGI compiler <= 20.4 to build your OpenMP applications, add -mp=libomp switch to use LLVM OpenMP runtime and enable OMPT based tracing. If you use Clang, make sure the LLVM OpenMP runtime library you link to was compiled with tools interface enabled.\nOnly a subset of the OMPT callbacks are processed:\nompt_callback_parallel_begin\nompt_callback_parallel_end\nompt_callback_sync_region\nompt_callback_task_create\nompt_callback_task_schedule\nompt_callback_implicit_task\nompt_callback_master\nompt_callback_reduction\nompt_callback_task_create\nompt_callback_cancel\nompt_callback_mutex_acquire\n,\nompt_callback_mutex_acquired\nompt_callback_mutex_acquired\n,\nompt_callback_mutex_released\nompt_callback_mutex_released\nompt_callback_work\nompt_callback_dispatch\nompt_callback_flush\nNote\nThe raw OMPT events are used to generate ranges indicating the runtime of OpenMP operations and constructs.\nExample screenshot:\nOS Runtime Libraries Trace\n\nOn Linux, OS runtime libraries can be traced to gather information about low-level userspace APIs. This traces the system call wrappers and thread synchronization interfaces exposed by the C runtime and POSIX Threads (pthread) libraries. This does not perform a complete runtime library API trace, but instead focuses on the functions that can take a\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 168000:180000]\n the “Insert NVTX Marker hotkey” option it is possible\n\nto add NVTX markers to a running non-console applications by pressing the\n\nF11 key. These will appear in the report under the NVTX Domain named “HotKey\n\nmarkers”.\nTypically calls to NVTX functions can be left in the source code even if the\n\napplication is not being built for profiling purposes, since the overhead is\n\nvery low when the profiler is not attached.\nNVTX is not intended to annotate very small pieces of code that are being called\n\nvery frequently. A good rule of thumb to use: if code being annotated usually\n\ntakes less than 1 microsecond to execute, adding an NVTX range around this code\n\nshould be done carefully.\nNote\nRange annotations should be matched carefully. If many ranges are opened but\n\nnot closed, Nsight Systems has no meaningful way to visualize it. A rule of\n\nthumb is to not have more than a couple dozen ranges open at any point in\n\ntime. Nsight Systems does not support reports with many unclosed ranges.\nNVTX Payloads and Counters (Preview)\nNVTX Extended Payloads and NVTX Counters increase the flexibility of NVTX\n\nannotations by allowing users to pass arbitrary structured data\n\nto NVTX events. This then will allow users to define the layout of this data in\n\nthe Nsight Systems UI without additional data conversion.\nFor more information, see\nNVTX documentation\n.\nNVTX Domains and Categories\nNVTX domains enable scoping of annotations. Unless specified differently, all\n\nevents and annotations are in the default domain. Additionally, categories can\n\nbe used to group events.\nNsight Systems gives the user the ability to include or exclude NVTX events from\n\na particular domain. This can be especially useful if you are profiling across\n\nmultiple libraries and are only interested in nvtx events from some of them.\nThis functionality is also available from the CLI. See the CLI documentation\n\nfor\n--nvtx-domain-include\nand\n--nvtx-domain-exclude\nfor more details.\nCategories that are set in by the user will be recognized and displayed in the\n\nGUI.\nCUDA Trace\n\nBasic CUDA trace\n\nNsight Systems is capable of capturing information about CUDA execution in the\n\nprofiled process.\nThe following information can be collected and presented on the timeline in the\n\nreport:\nCUDA API trace — trace of CUDA Runtime and CUDA Driver calls made by the application.\nCUDA Runtime calls typically start with\ncuda\nprefix (e.g.\ncudaLaunch\n).\nCUDA Driver calls typically start with\ncu\nprefix (e.g.\ncuDeviceGetCount\n).\nCUDA workload trace — trace of activity happening on the GPU, which includes\n\nmemory operations (e.g., Host-to-Device memory copies) and kernel executions.\n\nWithin the threads that use the CUDA API, additional child rows will appear\n\nin the timeline tree.\nOn Nsight Systems Workstation Edition, cuDNN and cuBLAS API tracing and OpenACC tracing.\nNear the bottom of the timeline row tree, the GPU node will appear and contain\n\na CUDA node. Within the CUDA node, each CUDA context used within the process\n\nwill be shown along with its corresponding CUDA streams. Steams will contain\n\nmemory operations and kernel launches on the GPU. Kernel launches are\n\nrepresented by blue, while memory transfers are displayed in red.\nThe easiest way to capture CUDA information is to launch the process from\n\nNsight Systems, and it will setup the environment for you. To do so, simply set\n\nup a normal launch and select the\nCollect CUDA trace\ncheckbox.\nFor Nsight Systems Workstation Edition this looks like:\nFor Nsight Systems Embedded Platforms Edition this looks like:\nAdditional configuration parameters are available:\nCollect backtraces for API calls longer than X seconds\n- turns on\n\ncollection of CUDA API backtraces and sets the minimum time a CUDA API event\n\nmust take before its backtraces are collected. Setting this value too low can\n\ncause high application overhead and seriously increase the size of your\n\nresults file.\nFlush data periodically\n— specifies the period after which an attempt to\n\nflush CUDA trace data will be made. Normally, in order to collect full CUDA\n\ntrace, the application needs to finalize the device used for CUDA work (call\ncudaDeviceReset()\n, and then let the application gracefully exit (as\n\nopposed to crashing).\nThis option allows flushing CUDA trace data even before the device is\n\nfinalized. However, it might introduce additional overhead to a random CUDA\n\nDriver or CUDA Runtime API call.\nSkip some API calls\n— avoids tracing insignificant CUDA Runtime API calls\n\n(namely,\ncudaConfigureCall()\n,\ncudaSetupArgument()\n,\ncudaHostGetDevicePointers()\n). Not tracing these functions allows\n\nNsight Systems to significantly reduce the profiling overhead, without losing\n\nany interesting data.\nCollect GPU Memory Usage\n- collects information used to generate a graph\n\nof CUDA allocated memory across time. Note that this will increase overhead.\n\nSee\nCUDA GPU Memory Allocation Graph\n.\nCollect Unified Memory CPU page faults\n- collects information on page\n\nfaults that occur when CPU code tries to access a memory page that resides on\n\nthe device. See\nUnified Memory CPU Page Faults\n.\nCollect Unified Memory GPU page faults\n- collects information on page\n\nfaults that occur when GPU code tries to access a memory page that resides on\n\nthe CPU. See\nUnified Memory GPU Page Faults\n.\nCollect CUDA Graph trace\n- by default, CUDA tracing will collect and\n\nexpose information on a whole graph basis. The user can opt to collect on a\n\nnode per node basis. See\nCUDA Graph Trace\n.\nFor Nsight Systems Workstation Edition,\nCollect cuDNN trace\n,\nCollect cuBLAS trace\n,\nCollect OpenACC trace\n- selects which (if any) extra libraries that\n\ndepend on CUDA to trace.\nOpenACC versions 2.0, 2.5, and 2.6 are supported when using PGI runtime\n\nversion 15.7 or greater and not compiling statically. In order to differentiate\n\nconstructs, a PGI runtime of 16.1 or later is required. Note that\n\nNsight Systems Workstation Edition does not support the GCC implementation of OpenACC at this\n\ntime.\nNote\nIf your application crashes before all collected CUDA trace data has been\n\ncopied out, some or all data might be lost and not present in the report.\nNote\nNsight Systems will not have information about CUDA events that were still in\n\ndevice buffers when analysis terminated. It is a good idea, if using\n\ncudaProfilerAPI to control analysis to call cudaDeviceReset before ending\n\nanalysis.\nLaunching NVIDIA Nsight Compute from a CUDA Kernel\n\nAfter you have used CUDA trace in Nsight Systems to locate a potential problem\n\nkernel, you may want to run NVIDIA Nsight Compute on that specific kernel. Right\n\nclick on the kernel to bring up a menu.\nIf this is the first time that the user has selected this feature, then we show\n\nthe following dialog box to get their preferences:\nThe first option invokes the NVIDIA Nsight Compute UI with known parameters. It\n\nis the preferred option for local or remote profiling. The user must provide the\n\nlocation of the ncu-ui executable. Nsight Systems will verify that the path and\n\nexecutable are valid.\nThe second option is provided for the convenience of users who do not have\n\nNVIDIA Nsight Compute installed on the host system, but simply want the command\n\nline they can use to run the Nsight Compute on the remote target by themselves\n\nwithout much automation.\nIf the user selects the option to start the NCU UI, Nsight Systems invokes it\n\nwith any relevant parameters from the Nsight Systems run.\nThe screenshot below shows NCU UI invoked by Nsight Systems. The red circles\n\nindicate the parameters pre-populated by Nsight Systems. Users may modify any\n\nof these parameters before launching the application and profiling the selected\n\nkernel.\nCUDA GPU Memory Allocation Graph\n\nWhen the\nCollect GPU Memory Usage\noption is selected from the\nCollect CUDA\n\ntrace\noption set, Nsight Systems will track CUDA GPU memory allocations and\n\ndeallocations and present a graph of this information in the timeline. This is\n\nnot the same as the GPU memory graph generated during stutter analysis on the\n\nWindows target. See\nWindows GPU Memory Utilization\nBelow, in the report on the left, memory is allocated and freed during the\n\ncollection. In the report on the right, memory is allocated, but not freed\n\nduring the collection.\nHere is another example, where allocations are happening on multiple GPUs\nUnified Memory Transfer Trace\n\nFor Nsight Systems Workstation Edition, Unified Memory (also called Managed Memory) transfer trace is enabled automatically in Nsight Systems when CUDA trace is selected. It incurs no overhead in programs that do not perform any Unified Memory transfers. Data is displayed in the Managed Memory area of the timeline:\nHtoD transfer\nindicates the CUDA kernel accessed managed memory that was residing on the host, so the kernel execution paused and transferred the data to the device. Heavy traffic here will incur performance penalties in CUDA kernels, so consider using manual cudaMemcpy operations from pinned host memory instead.\nPtoP transfer\nindicates the CUDA kernel accessed managed memory that was residing on a different device, so the kernel execution paused and transferred the data to this device. Heavy traffic here will incur performance penalties, so consider using manual cudaMemcpyPeer operations to transfer from other devices’ memory instead. The row showing these events is for the destination device - the source device is shown in the tooltip for each transfer event.\nDtoH transfer\nindicates the CPU accessed managed memory that was residing on a CUDA device, so the CPU execution paused and transferred the data to system memory. Heavy traffic here will incur performance penalties in CPU code, so consider using manual cudaMemcpy operations from pinned host memory instead.\nSome Unified Memory transfers are highlighted with red to indicate potential performance issues:\nTransfers with the following migration causes are highlighted:\nCoherence\nUnified Memory migration occurred to guarantee data coherence. SMs (streaming multiprocessors) stop until the migration completes.\nEviction\nUnified Memory migrated to the CPU because it was evicted to make room for another block of memory on the GPU. This happens due to memory overcommitment which is available on Linux with Compute Capability ≥ 6.\nUnified Memory CPU Page Faults\n\nThe Unified Memory CPU page faults feature in Nsight Systems tracks the page\n\nfaults that occur when CPU code tries to access a memory page that resides on\n\nthe device.\nNote\nCollecting Unified Memory CPU page faults can cause overhead of up to 70% in\n\ntesting. Please use this functionality only when needed.\nUnified Memory GPU Page Faults\n\nThe Unified Memory GPU page faults feature in Nsight Systems tracks the page\n\nfaults that occur when GPU code tries to access a memory page that resides on\n\nthe host.\nNote\nCollecting Unified Memory GPU page faults can cause overhead of up to 70% in\n\ntesting. Please use this functionality only when needed.\nCUDA Graph Trace\n\nNsight Systems is capable of capturing information about CUDA graphs in your\n\napplication at either the graph or node granularity. This can be set in the CLI\n\nusing the\n--cuda-graph-trace\noption, or in the GUI by setting the appropriate\n\ndrop down.\nWhen CUDA graph trace is set to\ngraph\n, the users sees each graph as one item\n\non the timeline:\nWhen CUDA graph trace is set to\nnode\n, the users sees each graph as a set of\n\nnodes on the timeline:\nTracing CUDA graphs at the graph level rather than the tracing the underlying\n\nnodes results in significantly less overhead. This option is only available\n\nith CUDA driver 515.43 or higher.\nCUDA Python Backtrace\n\nNsight Systems for Arm server (SBSA) platforms and x86 Linux targets, is capable of capturing Python shown here for clarity and that carriage\n\nreturns and indents were added to avoid wrapping documentation.\nStatistical Analysis\n\nStatistical Reports Shipped With Nsight Systems\n\nThe Nsight Systems development team created and maintains a set of report\n\nscripts for some of the commonly requested statistical reports. These scripts\n\nwill be updated to adapt to any changes in SQLite schema or internal data structures.\nThese scripts are located in the Nsight Systems package in the\n\nTarget-<architecture>/reports directory. The following standard reports are\n\navailable:\nNote:\nThe ability to display mangled names is a recent addition to the report\n\nfile format, and requires that the profile data be captured with a recent version\n\nof Nsys. Re-exporting an existing report file is not sufficient. If the raw,\n\nmangled kernel name data is not available, the default demangled names will be\n\nused.\nNote:\nAll time values given in nanoseconds by default. If you wish to output\n\nthe results using a different time unit, use the\n--timeunit\noption when\n\nrunning the recipe.\ncuda_api_gpu_sum[:base] – CUDA Summary (API/Kernels/MemOps)\n\nArguments\nbase : Optional argument, if given will cause summary to be over the base\n\nname of the kernel, rather than the templated name.\nOutput: All time values given in nanoseconds\nTime : Percentage of Total Time\nTotal Time : Total time used by all executions of this kernel\nInstances: Number of executions of this object\nAvg : Average execution time of this kernel\nMed : Median execution time of this kernel\nMin : Smallest execution time of this kernel\nMax : Largest execution time of this kernel\nStdDev : Standard deviation of execution time of this kernel\nCategory : Category of the operation\nOperation : Name of the kernel\nThis report provides a summary of CUDA API calls, kernels and memory operations,\n\nand their execution times. Note that the Time column is calculated using a\n\nsummation of the Total Time column, and represents that API call’s, kernel’s,\n\nor memory operation’s percent of the execution time of the APIs, kernels and\n\nmemory operations listed, and not a percentage of the application wall or CPU\n\nexecution time.\nThis report combines data from the cuda_api_sum, cuda_gpu_kern_sum, and\n\ncuda_gpu_mem_size_sum reports. It is very similar to profile section of\n\nnvprof –dependency-analysis.\ncuda_api_sum – CUDA API Summary\n\nArguments - None\nOutput: All time values given in nanoseconds\nTime : Percentage of Total Time\nTotal Time : Total time used by all executions of this function\nNum Calls : Number of calls to this function\nAvg : Average execution time of this function\nMed : Median execution time of this function\nMin : Smallest execution time of this function\nMax : Largest execution time of this function\nStdDev : Standard deviation of the time of this function\nName : Name of the function\nThis report provides a summary of CUDA API functions and their execution times.\n\nNote that the Time column is calculated using a summation of the Total Time\n\ncolumn, and represents that function’s percent of the execution time of the\n\nfunctions listed, and not a percentage of the application wall or CPU execution\n\ntime.\ncuda_api_trace – CUDA API Trace\n\nArguments - None\nOutput: All time values given in nanoseconds\nStart : Timestamp when API call was made\nDuration : Length of API calls\nName : API function name\nResult : return value of API call\nCorrID : Correlation used to map to other CUDA calls\nPid : Process ID that made the call\nTid : Thread ID that made the call\nT-Pri : Run priority of call thread\nThread Name : Name of thread that called API function\nThis report provides a trace record of CUDA API function calls and their execution times.\ncuda_gpu_kern_sum[:base] – CUDA GPU Kernel Summary\n\nArguments\nbase : Optional argument, if given, will cause summary to be over the base\n\nname of the kernel, rather than the templated name.\nOutput: All time values given in nanoseconds\nTime : Percentage of Total Time\nTotal Time : Total time used by all executions of this kernel\nInstances : Number of calls to this kernel\nAvg : Average execution time of this kernel\nMin : Smallest execution time of this kernel\nMax : Largest execution time of this kernel\nStdDev : Standard deviation of the time of this kernel\nName : Name of the kernel\nThis report provides a summary of CUDA kernels and their execution times. Note\n\nthat the Time column is calculated using a summation of the Total Time column,\n\nand represents that kernel’s percent of the execution time of the kernels listed,\n\nand not a percentage of the application wall or CPU execution time.\ncuda_gpu_mem_size_sum – CUDA GPU MemOps Summary (by Size)\n\nArguments - None\nOutput: All memory values given in KiB\nTotal : Total number of KiB utilized by this operation\nOperations : Number of executions of this operation\nAvg : Average memory size of this operation\nMin : Smallest memory size of this operation\nMax : Largest memory size of this operation\nStdDev : Standard deviation of execution time of this operation\nName : Name of the operation\nThis report provides a summary of GPU memory operations and the amount of memory\n\nthey utilize.\ncuda_gpu_mem_time_sum – CUDA GPU MemOps Summary (by Time)\n\nArguments - None\nOutput: All memory values given in KiB\nTime : Percentage of Total Time\nTotal Time : Total time used by all executions of this operation\nOperations: Number of operations of this type\nAvg : Average execution time of this operation\nMin : Smallest execution time of this operation\nMax : Largest execution time of this operation\nStdDev : Standard deviation of execution time of this operation\nOperation : Name of the memory operation\nThis report provides a summary of GPU memory operations and their execution\n\ntimes. Note that the Time column is calculated using a summation of the Total\n\nTime column, and represents that operation’s percent of the execution time of\n\nthe operations listed, and not a percentage of the application wall or CPU\n\nexecution time.\ncuda_gpu_sum[:base] – CUDA GPU Summary (Kernels/MemOps)\n\nArguments\nbase : Optional argument, if given, will cause summary to be over the base\n\nname of the kernel, rather than the templated name.\nOutput: All time values given in nanoseconds\nTime : Percentage of Total Time\nTotal Time : Total time used by all executions of this kernel\nInstances : Number of executions of this object\nAvg : Average execution time of this kernel\nMin : Smallest execution time of this kernel\nMax : Largest execution time of this kernel\nStdDev : Standard deviation of execution time of this kernel\nCategory : Category of the operation\nName : Name of the kernel\nThis report provides a summary of CUDA kernels and memory operations, and their\n\nexecution times. Note that the Time column is calculated using a summation of\n\nthe Total Time column, and represents that kernel’s or memory operation’s percent\n\nof the execution time of the kernels and memory operations listed, and not a\n\npercentage of the application wall or CPU execution time.\nThis report combines data from the cuda_gpu_kern_sum and cuda_gpu_mem_time_sum\n\nreports. This report is very similar to output of the command nvprof\n\n–print-gpu-summary.\ncuda_gpu_trace – CUDA GPU Trace\n\nArguments - None\nOutput:\nStart : Start time of trace event in seconds\nDuration : Length of event in nanoseconds\nCorrId : Correlation ID\nGrdX, GrdY, GrdZ : Grid values\nBlkX, BlkY, BlkZ : Block values\nReg/Trd : Registers per thread\nStcSMem : Size of Static Shared Memory\nDymSMem : Size of Dynamic Shared Memory\nBytes : Size of memory operation\nThru : Throughput in MB per Second\nSrcMemKd : Memcpy source memory kind or memset memor\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 464000:468000]\nup becoming\n\nsynchronous if the memory is pageable. This rule is not applicable for Nsight\n\nSystems Embedded Platforms Edition\nSuggestion: If applicable, use pinned memory instead\nSynchronous Memcpy\nThis rule identifies synchronous memory transfers that block the host.\nSuggestion: Use cudaMemcpy*Async APIs instead.\nSynchronous Memset\nThis rule identifies synchronous memset operations that block the host.\nSuggestion: Use cudaMemset*Async APIs instead.\nSynchronization APIs\nThis rule identifies synchronization APIs that block the host until all issued\n\nCUDA calls are complete.\nSuggestions: Avoid excessive use of synchronization. Use asynchronous CUDA event\n\ncalls, such as cudaStreamWaitEvent and cudaEventSynchronize, to prevent host\n\nsynchronization.\nGPU Low Utilization Rules\n\nNsight Systems determines GPU utilization based on API trace data in the\n\ncollection. Current rules consider CUDA, Vulkan, DX12, and OpenGL API use of the\n\nGPU.\nGPU Starvation\nThis rule identifies time ranges where a GPU is idle for longer than 500ms. The\n\nthreshold is adjustable.\nSuggestions: Use CPU sampling data, OS Runtime blocked state backtraces, and/or\n\nOS Runtime APIs related to thread synchronization to understand if a sluggish or\n\nblocked CPU is causing the gaps. Add NVTX annotations to CPU code to understand\n\nthe reason behind the gaps.\nNotes: For each process, each GPU is examined, and gaps are found within the\n\ntime range that starts with the beginning of the first GPU operation on that\n\ndevice and ends with the end of the last GPU operation on that device. GPU gaps\n\nthat cannot be addressed by the user are excluded. This includes:\nProfiling overhead in the middle of a GPU gap.\nThe initial gap in the report that is seen before the first GPU operation.\nThe final gap that is seen after the last GPU operation.\nGPU Low Utilization\nThis rule identifies time regions with low utilization.\nSuggestions: Use CPU sampling data, OS Runtime blocked state backtraces, and/or\n\nOS Runtime APIs related to thread synchronization to understand if a sluggish or\n\nblocked CPU is causing the gaps. Add NVTX annotations to CPU code to understand\n\nthe reason behind the gaps.\nNotes: For each process, each GPU is examined, and gaps are found within the\n\ntime range that starts with the beginning of the first GPU operation on that\n\ndevice and ends with the end of the last GPU operation on that device. This time\n\nrange is then divided into equal chunks, and the GPU utilization is calculated\n\nfor each chunk. The utilization includes all GPU operations as well as profiling\n\noverheads that the user cannot address.\nThe utilization refers to the “time” utilization and not the “resource”\n\nutilization. This rule attempts to find time gaps when the GPU is or isn’t being\n\nused, but does not take into account how many GPU resources are being used.\n\nTherefore, a single running memcpy is considered the same amount of “utilization”\n\nas a huge kernel that takes over all the cores. If multiple operations run\n\nconcurrently in the same chunk, their utilization will be added up and may\n\nexceed 100%.\nChunks with an in-use percentage less than the threshold value are displayed. If\n\nconsecutive chunks have a low in-use percentage, the individual chunks are\n\ncoalesced into a single display record, keeping the weighted average of\n\npercentages. This is why returned chunks may have different durations.\nMulti-Report Analysis\n\nNsight Systems Multi-Report Analysis is functionality to better support complex\n\nstatistical analysis across multiple result files. Possible use cases for this\n\nfunctionality include:\nMulti-Node Analysis - When you run Nsight Systems across a cluster, it\n\ntypically generates one result file per rank on the cluster. Although not limited to\ntracing, context propagation allows [traces](../signals/traces/) to build causal\ninformation about a system across services that are arbitrarily distributed\nacross process and network boundaries.\n\nTo understand context propagation, you need to understand two separate concepts:\ncontext and propagation.\n\n## Context\n\nContext is an object that contains the information for the sending and receiving\nservice, or [execution unit](/docs/specs/otel/glossary/#execution-unit), to\ncorrelate one signal with another.\n\nWhen Service A calls Service B, Service A includes a trace ID and a span ID as\npart of the context. Service B uses these values to create a new span that\nbelongs to the same trace, setting the span from Service A as its parent. This\nmakes it possible to track the full flow of a request across service boundaries.\n\n## Propagation\n\nPropagation is the mechanism that moves context between services and processes.\nIt serializes or deserializes the context object and provides the relevant\ninformation to be propagated from one service to another.\n\nPropagation is usually handled by instrumentation libraries and is transparent\nto the user. In the event that you need to manually propagate context, you can\nuse the [Propagators API](/docs/specs/otel/context/api-propagators/).\n\nOpenTelemetry maintains several official propagators. The default propagator\nuses the headers specified by the\n[W3C TraceContext](https://www.w3.org/TR/trace-context/) specification.\n\n## Example\n\nA service called `Frontend` that provides different HTTP endpoints such as\n`POST /cart/add` and `GET /checkout/` reaches out to a downstream service\n`Product Catalog` via an HTTP endpoint `GET /product` to receive details on\nproducts that a user wants to add to the cart or that are part of the checkout.\nTo understand activities in the `Product Catalog` service within the context of\nrequests coming from `Frontend`, the context (here: Trace ID and Span ID as\n\"Parent ID\") is propagated using the `traceparent` header as it is defined in\nthe W3C TraceContext specification. This means the IDs are embedded in the\nfields of the header:\n\n```text\n<version>-<trace-id>-<parent-id>-<trace-flags>\n```\n\nFor example:\n\n```text\n00-a0892f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-f03067aa0ba902b7-01\n```\n\n### Traces\n\nAs mentioned, context propagation allows traces to build causal information\nacross services. In this example, the two calls to the HTTP endpoint\n`GET /product` of service `Product Catalog` can be correlated with their\nupstream calls in service `Frontend` by extracting the remote context from the\n`traceparent` header and injecting it into the local context to set the Trace ID\nand Parent ID. With that, it is possible in a [backend](/ecosystem/vendors) like\n[Jaeger](https://jaegertracing.io) to see two requests as spans of one trace.\n\n![Context propagation example showing trace correlation across services](context-propagation-example.svg)\n\n### Logs\n\nOpenTelemetry SDKs are able to automatically correlate logs with traces. This\nmeans they can inject context (Trace ID, Span ID) into a log record. This not\nonly enables you to see logs in the context of the trace and span they belong\nto, but it also enables you to see logs that belong together across service or\nexecution unit boundaries.\n\n### Metrics\n\nIn the case of metrics, context propagation enables you to aggregate\nmeasurements in that context. For example, instead of only looking at the\nresponse time of all the `GET /product` requests, you can also get metrics for\ncombinations of `POST /cart/add > GET /product` and\n`GET /checkout < GET /product`.\n\n| Name                            | Calls Per Second | Average Response Time |\n| ------------------------------- | ---------------- | --------------------- |\n| `* > GET /product`              | 370              | 300ms                 |\n| `POST /card/add > GET /product` | 330              | 130ms                 |\n| `GET /checkout > GET /product`  | 40               | 1703ms                |\n\n## Custom Context Propagation\n\nFor most use cases, you will find\n[instrumentation libraries or native library instrumentation](/docs/concepts/instrumentation/libraries/)\nthat handle the context propagation for you. In some cases no such support is\navailable and you want to create that support for yourself. To do so you need to\nleverage the previously mentioned Propagators API:\n\n- On the side of the sender, the context is\n  [injected](/docs/specs/otel/context/api-propagators/#inject) into the carrier,\n  for example, into the headers of an HTTP request. In other cases, you need to\n  find a place that can store metadata for your request.\n- On the receiving side, the context is\n  [extracted](/docs/specs/otel/context/api-propagators/#extract) from the\n  carrier. Again, in the case of HTTP, this is retrieved from the headers. In\n  other cases, you pick the place you chose on the sending side to store the\n  context.\n\nNote that it is possible to propagate context in protocols that do not have a\ndedicated field for metadata, but you have to make sure that on the receiving\nside they are extracted and removed before the data is processed, otherwise you\nmay create undefined behavior.\n\nFor the following languages a step-by-step tutorial exists for custom context\npropagation:\n\n- [Erlang](/docs/languages/erlang/propagation/#manual-context-propagation)\n- [JavaScript](/docs/languages/js/propagation/#manual-context-propagation)\n- [PHP](/docs/languages/php/propagation/#manual-context-propagation)\n- [Python](/docs/languages/python/propagation/#manual-context-propagation)\n\n## Security best practices\n\nPropagation involves sending and receiving data across service boundaries, which\ncan have security implications.\n\n### External services\n\nWhen your service interacts with external services (services you do not own or\ntrust), consider the following:\n\n- **Incoming context**: Be cautious when accepting context from external\n  sources. Malicious actors could send forged trace headers to manipulate your\n  tracing data or potentially exploit vulnerabilities in context parsing. You\n  might want to ignore or sanitize incoming context from untrusted sources.\n- **Outgoing context**: Be mindful of what you propagate to external services.\n  Internal trace IDs, span IDs, or baggage items might reveal sensitive\n  information about your internal architecture or business logic. You may want\n  to configure your propagators to not send context to external or public-facing\n  endpoints.\n\n### Baggage\n\n[Baggage](../signals/baggage/) allows you to propagate arbitrary key-value\npairs. Since this data is propagated across service boundaries, avoid putting\nsensitive information (like user credentials, API keys, or PII) in baggage, as\nit might be logged or sent to untrusted downstream services.\n\n## Support in Language SDKs\n\nFor the individual language-specific implementations of the OpenTelemetry API &\nSDK, you will find details on the support of context propagation in the\nrespective documentation pages:\n\n- [C++](/docs/languages/cpp/instrumentation/#context-propagation)\n- .NET\n- [Erlang](/docs/languages/erlang/propagation/)\n- [Go](/docs/languages/go/instrumentation/#propagators-and-context)\n- [Java](/docs/languages/java/api/#context-api)\n- [JavaScript](/docs/languages/js/propagation/)\n- [PHP](/docs/languages/php/propagation/)\n- [Python](/docs/languages/python/propagation/)\n- [Ruby](/docs/languages/ruby/instrumentation/#context-propagation)\n- Rust\n- Swift\n\n> [!IMPORTANT] Help wanted\n>\n> For languages .NET, Rust, and Swift, the language-specific documentation for\n> context propagation is missing. If you know any of those languages and are\n> interested to help, [learn how you can contribute](/docs/contributing/)!\n\n## Specification\n\nTo learn more about context propagation, see the\n[Context specification](/docs/specs/otel/context/).\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 8256]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 8256, "selected_characters": 8256, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-RDMA/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "9f4bea8bc130fc7dcdf0b53d20ce7269555cddd2a7c67e5d54c6dbd7a55b2720", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "===== libibverbs/man/ibv_post_send.3 =====\n.\\\" -*- nroff -*-\n.\\\" Licensed under the OpenIB.org BSD license (FreeBSD Variant) - See COPYING.md\n.\\\"\n.TH IBV_POST_SEND 3 2006-10-31 libibverbs \"Libibverbs Programmer's Manual\"\n.SH \"NAME\"\nibv_post_send \\- post a list of work requests (WRs) to a send queue\n.SH \"SYNOPSIS\"\n.nf\n.B #include <infiniband/verbs.h>\n.sp\n.BI \"int ibv_post_send(struct ibv_qp \" \"*qp\" \", struct ibv_send_wr \" \"*wr\" ,\n.BI \"                  struct ibv_send_wr \" \"**bad_wr\" );\n.fi\n.SH \"DESCRIPTION\"\n.B ibv_post_send()\nposts the linked list of work requests (WRs) starting with\n.I wr\nto the send queue of the queue pair\n.I qp\\fR.\nIt stops processing WRs from this list at the first failure (that can\nbe detected immediately while requests are being posted), and returns\nthis failing WR through\n.I bad_wr\\fR.\n.PP\nThe argument\n.I wr\nis an ibv_send_wr struct, as defined in <infiniband/verbs.h>.\n.PP\n.nf\nstruct ibv_send_wr {\n.in +8\nuint64_t                wr_id;
